Table 10
Constructive Confrontation, Understanding Deception, and Managing Dishonesty
1. Q: What does it really mean to confront someone suspected of lying, and why should confrontation not be treated as aggression?
A: Show answer
Confronting someone suspected of dishonesty is a process of seeking clarity, examining inconsistencies, understanding possible motives, and deciding how to address the situation. It should not begin with an assumption that the other person is guilty. An aggressive confrontation focuses on winning an argument, embarrassing the other person, or forcing an admission. A constructive confrontation focuses on understanding what happened and determining whether trust can be restored. The distinction matters because people may become defensive when they feel attacked, making it harder to obtain reliable information. A productive conversation therefore requires preparation, emotional control, respectful questioning, and an appropriate opportunity for both parties to explain their perspectives.

Practical scenario: An employee discovers that a colleague provided inaccurate information about completing a report. Instead of immediately accusing the colleague of lying, the employee requests a private discussion and asks for clarification about the outstanding work.

Key point:
- Confrontation should seek understanding and accountability rather than humiliation.
- Suspicion should initiate investigation, not automatically establish guilt.
- Respectful communication can preserve the possibility of resolving the issue.

Source links:
- Eliciting Reliable Information in Investigative Interviews — Vrij, Hope and Fisher (2014)
- Investigative Interviewing: A Review of the Literature and a Model of Science-Based Practice — Oxford Handbook (2023)
2. Q: Why should suspicion of dishonesty be distinguished from evidence that someone has actually lied?
A: Show answer
Suspicion often develops because a person's statements appear inconsistent, incomplete, or different from information already available. However, inconsistency does not automatically establish intentional deception. People can misunderstand questions, forget details, make mistakes, or communicate poorly. A reliable assessment therefore separates observable facts from personal interpretations. Before confronting someone, identify the original statement, the conflicting information, the source of the evidence, and the possible alternative explanations. Avoid turning an uncomfortable feeling into a definitive accusation. Research on deception judgments demonstrates that unaided human lie detection is relatively limited. A major meta-analysis by Bond and DePaulo found average accuracy of approximately 54% across the studies examined, illustrating why intuition alone is insufficient.

Practical scenario: A manager discovers that an employee's explanation of a delayed payment differs from the bank record. The manager first verifies the transaction date, payment reference, and communication history before deciding whether the discrepancy indicates deception or a misunderstanding.

Key point:
- Suspicion is not proof of dishonesty.
- Evidence should be independently checked before accusations are made.
- Alternative explanations must be considered.

Source links:
- Accuracy of Deception Judgments — Bond and DePaulo (2006)
- Cues to Deception — DePaulo et al. (2003)
3. Q: Why do people sometimes lie to protect themselves from punishment, embarrassment, or personal consequences?
A: Show answer
Self-preservation is one possible motivation behind deceptive behavior. People may conceal mistakes because they fear disciplinary action, rejection, criticism, financial loss, or damage to their reputation. The immediate objective is often to avoid an uncomfortable consequence rather than to create a permanent false identity. However, a small defensive lie can develop into a larger problem when the person repeatedly adds explanations to protect the original statement. Understanding this motivation helps the person conducting the conversation recognize the pressure involved without automatically excusing the behavior. The objective should be to establish what happened, determine the impact, and identify whether the person is prepared to accept responsibility.

Practical scenario: An employee accidentally sends an incomplete financial report. Afraid of being criticized, the employee tells the supervisor that the report was fully checked. When the error is discovered, the supervisor investigates the original mistake and the subsequent misrepresentation separately.

Key point:
- Fear of consequences can motivate concealment.
- An initial mistake and a subsequent deliberate misrepresentation are separate issues.
- Accountability should focus on both the underlying error and its consequences.

Source links:
- Truth-Default Theory and the Psychology of Lying and Deception Detection — Levine (2022)
- Eliciting Reliable Information in Investigative Interviews — Vrij, Hope and Fisher (2014)
4. Q: How can social pressure encourage people to create false stories about their identity, achievements, or circumstances?
A: Show answer
People frequently experience pressure to meet expectations established by their families, professional groups, social circles, or communities. When acceptance depends heavily on status, success, or appearance, someone may exaggerate achievements or conceal information that could make them appear unsuccessful. Such behavior can become a method of managing social identity. The individual may initially believe that the deception is necessary to maintain acceptance, but repeated exaggeration creates additional pressure because the person must continue supporting the invented story. Recognizing social pressure as a possible motive helps explain why deception can occur even when there is no immediate financial benefit. Nevertheless, understanding the pressure does not remove responsibility for misleading others.

Practical scenario: Someone tells prospective colleagues that they have extensive management experience despite having held only an entry-level position. The purpose is to gain acceptance and appear professionally competitive.

Key point:
- Social expectations can influence dishonest behavior.
- Status-based deception can become difficult to maintain.
- Personal insecurity may explain behavior but does not automatically justify it.

Source links:
- Truth-Default Theory and the Psychology of Lying and Deception Detection — Levine (2022)
- Cues to Deception — DePaulo et al. (2003)
5. Q: What is an altruistic lie, and how does it differ from deception intended to obtain personal benefits?
A: Show answer
An altruistic lie is a misleading statement presented with the intention of protecting another person's feelings, reducing emotional discomfort, or avoiding unnecessary conflict. A common example is giving an overly positive opinion about a harmless personal decision. This differs from deception intended to obtain money, influence, professional advantage, or control over another person. However, intention alone does not determine whether a lie is harmless. Even a supposedly protective statement can prevent someone from receiving important information or making an informed decision. A useful assessment considers the intention, the information concealed, the actual consequences, and whether the other person had a legitimate need to know the truth.

Practical scenario: A friend says that a presentation was excellent despite noticing several weaknesses. The intention may be encouragement rather than manipulation. However, if the presenter is preparing for an important professional assessment, constructive and honest feedback would be more useful.

Key point:
- Not all lies have the same intention or consequences.
- Protective intentions do not automatically make deception harmless.
- Honest feedback should be adapted to the situation without withholding essential information.

Source links:
- Cues to Deception — DePaulo et al. (2003)
- Eliciting Reliable Information in Investigative Interviews — Vrij, Hope and Fisher (2014)
6. Q: Why is personal gain considered an important motive when examining deceptive behavior?
A: Show answer
Personal gain can motivate individuals to misrepresent facts to obtain financial resources, professional opportunities, authority, favorable treatment, or other advantages. Unlike a minor protective lie, deceptive behavior for personal gain may involve deliberate planning and repeated misrepresentation. The person may provide selected information, hide unfavorable facts, or create a narrative that makes an advantage appear legitimate. When examining possible self-interested deception, it is important to identify who benefits, who bears the risk, what information was withheld, and whether the decision-maker was given an accurate picture. The investigation should focus on the transaction and supporting evidence rather than relying exclusively on assumptions about personality.

Practical scenario: A supplier claims that additional services were completed and requests extra payment. The organization compares the contract, delivery records, approval documents, and evidence of actual work before approving the additional charge.

Key point:
- Personal advantage can create incentives for deliberate deception.
- Examine financial and practical benefits associated with the disputed statement.
- Verify claims against independent records.

Source links:
- Accuracy of Deception Judgments — Bond and DePaulo (2006)
- Eliciting Reliable Information in Investigative Interviews — Vrij, Hope and Fisher (2014)
7. Q: Why is understanding someone's motive different from accepting or excusing their dishonesty?
A: Show answer
Understanding motivation is an analytical step that helps identify why a person may have misrepresented information. It does not mean that the behavior should be accepted or that responsibility should disappear. A person might lie because of fear, shame, social pressure, personal advantage, or a desire to protect someone. Each explanation may require a different conversational approach, but the underlying facts and consequences still need to be addressed. Empathy allows a discussion to remain constructive, while accountability ensures that the affected person or organization is not expected to tolerate repeated misconduct. The strongest approach combines an understanding of human motivations with clear standards, evidence, and reasonable boundaries.

Practical scenario: A finance manager learns that an employee concealed a payment error because they feared criticism. The manager acknowledges the pressure but still requires correction of the records, explanation of the concealment, and safeguards against repetition.

Key point:
- Explanation is not the same as justification.
- Empathy and accountability can operate together.
- Corrective action should reflect the seriousness and impact of the behavior.

Source links:
- Eliciting Reliable Information in Investigative Interviews — Vrij, Hope and Fisher (2014)
- Investigative Interviewing — Oxford Handbook of Psychology and Law (2023)
8. Q: How should someone prepare before confronting a person suspected of serious dishonesty?
A: Show answer
Preparation should begin by defining the exact issue that needs clarification. Identify the disputed statement, relevant dates, supporting records, possible consequences, and the outcome you hope to achieve. Separate facts that are independently established from information that still needs confirmation. Consider whether the conversation should produce an explanation, a correction, an apology, a repayment, or a change in future behavior. Avoid entering the discussion with a predetermined accusation or an unrealistic expectation that the other person will immediately confess. Preparation also means choosing a suitable time and environment, considering safety and power differences, and deciding which facts should be discussed first. This prevents the conversation from becoming an emotional exchange without a clear objective.

Practical scenario: Before discussing an inaccurate procurement declaration, a manager reviews the purchase order, supplier invoice, delivery confirmation, and approval history. The manager prepares specific questions instead of relying on an informal accusation.

Key point:
- Establish the facts and objective before the conversation.
- Organize evidence chronologically.
- Decide what resolution would be appropriate.

Source links:
- Investigative Interviewing: A Review of the Literature and a Model of Science-Based Practice — Oxford Handbook (2023)
- Eliciting Reliable Information in Investigative Interviews — Vrij, Hope and Fisher (2014)
9. Q: How should someone decide whether a suspected lie is important enough to confront?
A: Show answer
Not every inaccurate statement deserves the same response. The decision should consider the seriousness of the information, the consequences for affected people, the potential for repeated deception, and the risks associated with confrontation. A minor personal matter may not justify a difficult discussion, especially when the other person has no obligation to disclose the information. However, dishonesty involving financial decisions, workplace responsibilities, safety, professional obligations, or significant personal commitments may require a response. The decision should also consider whether a constructive outcome is achievable. The purpose is not to challenge every questionable statement but to use judgment to determine when clarification, accountability, or protective action is necessary.

Practical scenario: A friend does not disclose that they changed their weekend plans. The matter may be insignificant. By contrast, a colleague concealing a serious procurement irregularity creates a risk that requires formal investigation.

Key point:
- The seriousness and consequences of the deception matter.
- Not every lie needs confrontation.
- Material dishonesty may require timely corrective action.

Source links:
- Truth-Default Theory and the Psychology of Lying and Deception Detection — Levine (2022)
- Investigative Interviewing — Oxford Handbook (2023)
10. Q: What is the difference between ignoring an insignificant lie and tolerating serious repeated deception?
A: Show answer
Ignoring an insignificant lie can sometimes be a reasonable way to prevent unnecessary conflict. However, ignoring repeated or harmful deception can allow damaging behavior to continue. The important distinction is whether the statement affects another person's rights, financial interests, safety, informed decisions, or ability to carry out responsibilities. A low-impact personal exaggeration may require little attention, whereas repeated false information in a professional setting may undermine accountability and create substantial risk. The decision should therefore be based on the significance of the issue rather than the emotional desire to prove that someone has lied. Patterns of behavior also deserve attention because repeated incidents may indicate a broader problem.

Practical scenario: A colleague exaggerates a minor personal achievement during informal conversation. Another colleague repeatedly alters project completion reports to conceal delays. The second situation requires verification and an appropriate management response.

Key point:
- Assess consequences rather than treating every lie equally.
- Repeated harmful deception requires stronger controls.
- Silence should be a considered decision, not an automatic response.

Source links:
- Truth-Default Theory and the Psychology of Lying and Deception Detection — Levine (2022)
- Investigative Interviewing — Oxford Handbook (2023)
11. Q: Why should a sensitive conversation take place in a neutral and comfortable environment?
A: Show answer
The environment can influence how participants experience a difficult conversation. A neutral location may reduce the sense that one participant controls the discussion or has an advantage over the other. A quiet environment also minimizes distractions and allows participants to explain their perspectives without an unnecessary audience. The selected location should provide privacy without creating intimidation or compromising safety. In professional situations, the appropriate environment may be a confidential meeting room or a formal interview setting. The objective is to create conditions that support clear communication, not to use the environment as a psychological technique for forcing an admission.

Practical scenario: Two colleagues disagree about an inaccurate project report. Their supervisor arranges a private meeting in a neutral meeting room instead of questioning one employee in front of the entire department.

Key point:
- Choose a private and appropriate environment.
- Avoid public confrontation and unnecessary embarrassment.
- Consider safety, confidentiality, and the participants' relative positions.

Source links:
- Eliciting Reliable Information in Investigative Interviews — Vrij, Hope and Fisher (2014)
- Investigative Interviewing — Oxford Handbook (2023)
12. Q: Why is active listening important when confronting someone about possible dishonesty?
A: Show answer
Active listening involves concentrating on the speaker's account, allowing them to finish, identifying the important details, and checking whether their explanation has been understood correctly. Listening does not mean agreeing with the person or accepting every statement. It means giving the person a genuine opportunity to explain events before making a conclusion. Interrupting, challenging every sentence, or immediately presenting counterarguments can reduce the amount of useful information obtained. A listener can summarize important points, ask for clarification, and compare the explanation with existing records afterward. Research on investigative interviewing emphasizes rapport, communication, and information gathering as important elements in obtaining more complete and reliable accounts.

Practical scenario: A project coordinator explains why a delivery report was submitted late. The manager listens to the full explanation, summarizes the timeline, and then asks about the differences between the account and the delivery records.

Key point:
- Allow the other person to explain their account.
- Listen before making a judgment.
- Summarize and clarify important information.

Source links:
- Eliciting Reliable Information in Investigative Interviews — Vrij, Hope and Fisher (2014)
- Investigative Interviewing — Oxford Handbook (2023)
13. Q: How can open body language support a difficult conversation, and why should it not be treated as proof of honesty?
A: Show answer
Relaxed posture, appropriate eye contact, and non-threatening gestures may communicate that someone is willing to listen. These behaviors can help make a conversation feel less hostile, but they cannot establish whether the speaker is telling the truth. People express stress, discomfort, and confidence differently depending on personality, culture, health, and circumstances. Someone who looks nervous may be honest, while someone who appears calm may be dishonest. Research examining deception cues has found that many visible behaviors have weak or inconsistent relationships with lying. Body language should therefore be used to support respectful communication, not as a diagnostic test for deception.

Practical scenario: During a discussion about a missing document, a manager maintains a calm posture and listens without intimidation. The manager does not interpret the employee's lack of eye contact as evidence of guilt.

Key point:
- Open posture can support constructive communication.
- Eye contact and gestures are not reliable standalone lie detectors.
- Evaluate statements through evidence and context.

Source links:
- Cues to Deception — DePaulo et al. (2003)
- Accuracy of Deception Judgments — Bond and DePaulo (2006)
14. Q: Why should accusations be replaced with statements that explain personal observations and feelings?
A: Show answer
Accusations can make a person feel attacked and encourage them to defend their reputation rather than explain what happened. A more constructive approach describes the specific situation and its effect on the speaker. Statements beginning with expressions such as "I noticed" or "I was concerned when" communicate an issue without immediately assigning dishonest intentions. This does not mean avoiding accountability or pretending that a serious problem is insignificant. It simply allows the conversation to begin with verifiable information rather than a personal judgment. Once the other person has responded, the discussion can move toward the evidence, consequences, and corrective action.

Practical scenario: Instead of saying, "You lied about sending the payment," a manager says, "I noticed that the payment confirmation is missing, although the report indicates that payment was completed. Could you explain the difference?"

Key point:
- Describe facts before assigning motives.
- Explain the impact of the discrepancy.
- Use non-accusatory language without weakening accountability.

Source links:
- Eliciting Reliable Information in Investigative Interviews — Vrij, Hope and Fisher (2014)
- Investigative Interviewing — Oxford Handbook (2023)
15. Q: Why should someone clearly communicate their intentions before discussing suspected dishonesty?
A: Show answer
Clearly explaining the purpose of a conversation helps reduce uncertainty about what the discussion is intended to achieve. The other person should understand whether the objective is to clarify an inconsistency, correct a mistake, rebuild trust, or establish accountability. Hiding the purpose behind misleading questions may create additional mistrust and make cooperation more difficult. Transparency does not require disclosing every investigative detail when a formal investigation is involved. It means communicating honestly about the nature and boundaries of the discussion. A clear objective also helps the person conducting the conversation stay focused and avoid turning a practical discussion into an emotional argument.

Practical scenario: A supervisor begins a meeting by explaining that the purpose is to understand discrepancies in a project expense report and agree on the appropriate correction, rather than to embarrass the employee.

Key point:
- Explain the legitimate purpose of the conversation.
- Avoid deceptive conversational tactics.
- Maintain transparency while respecting legitimate investigative confidentiality.

Source links:
- Eliciting Reliable Information in Investigative Interviews — Vrij, Hope and Fisher (2014)
- Investigative Interviewing — Oxford Handbook (2023)
16. Q: Why are open-ended questions generally more useful than leading or accusatory questions when seeking clarification?
A: Show answer
Open-ended questions invite the other person to provide explanations in their own words. They allow additional information to emerge that may not have been considered when the question was prepared. Leading questions, by contrast, may encourage the person to agree with an assumption embedded in the question. Accusatory questions can also limit discussion because the individual may concentrate on defending themselves. Questions should focus on specific events, timelines, decisions, and supporting information. Research on investigative interviewing supports information-gathering approaches that use rapport and open-ended exploratory questions rather than relying primarily on accusatorial questioning.

Practical scenario: Instead of asking, "You deliberately changed the figures, didn't you?" a finance manager asks, "Could you explain how these figures were prepared and why they differ from the original records?"

Key point:
- Open-ended questions encourage fuller explanations.
- Avoid embedding an accusation in the question.
- Follow general questions with specific factual clarification.

Source links:
- Eliciting Reliable Information in Investigative Interviews — Vrij, Hope and Fisher (2014)
- Investigative Interviewing — Oxford Handbook (2023)
17. Q: How should confidentiality be discussed during a sensitive conversation about dishonesty?
A: Show answer
Confidentiality can make people more comfortable discussing sensitive matters, but it should never be promised without considering the circumstances. In personal relationships, the participants may agree that the discussion will remain private. In professional settings, however, information may need to be reported to authorized managers, investigators, auditors, safeguarding personnel, or other responsible parties. Legal and safety obligations may also limit confidentiality. The person initiating the discussion should explain who may receive the information and why. A promise that cannot realistically be honored could create another breach of trust. Appropriate privacy protection means limiting unnecessary disclosure while preserving legitimate reporting and accountability requirements.

Practical scenario: An employee discusses a suspected financial irregularity with a manager. The manager explains that the information will be restricted to authorized personnel but may need to be shared with the internal investigation or compliance team.

Key point:
- Protect sensitive information.
- Do not promise absolute confidentiality when exceptions may apply.
- Explain legitimate reporting boundaries before collecting sensitive disclosures.

Source links:
- Investigative Interviewing — Oxford Handbook (2023)
- Eliciting Reliable Information in Investigative Interviews — Vrij, Hope and Fisher (2014)
18. Q: How can empathy encourage honesty without allowing another person to avoid responsibility?
A: Show answer
Empathy involves attempting to understand another person's emotions, pressures, and perspective. When discussing suspected deception, it can help reveal circumstances that may explain the person's behavior. Someone who fears punishment may be more willing to describe a mistake if the conversation does not begin with humiliation. However, empathy should not eliminate the need to establish facts or correct harmful conduct. A balanced approach recognizes the emotional difficulty of the situation while maintaining clear expectations about honesty and accountability. This combination can support a more useful conversation and help identify the practical changes needed to prevent similar incidents.

Practical scenario: A team leader learns that an employee concealed a missed deadline because of excessive workload. The leader acknowledges the pressure, investigates the inaccurate report, and establishes a more realistic scheduling and reporting procedure.

Key point:
- Empathy helps people discuss difficult circumstances.
- Understanding does not remove responsibility.
- Constructive support should accompany appropriate corrective action.

Source links:
- Eliciting Reliable Information in Investigative Interviews — Vrij, Hope and Fisher (2014)
- Investigative Interviewing — Oxford Handbook (2023)
19. Q: What role does emotional intelligence play when confronting someone suspected of lying?
A: Show answer
Emotional intelligence involves recognizing emotions, understanding how they influence behavior, and responding appropriately to emotional situations. During a difficult conversation, it helps the person conducting the discussion recognize their own anger, frustration, anxiety, and expectations. It also encourages attention to the other person's emotional experience without assuming that every emotional response has a particular meaning. Someone may become defensive because of embarrassment, fear, or disagreement, not necessarily because they are lying. Emotional intelligence is therefore useful for managing communication and maintaining perspective, but it should not be treated as a special ability to identify deception accurately.

Practical scenario: A supervisor feels angry after discovering an inconsistent report. Instead of reacting immediately, the supervisor pauses, reviews the evidence, and schedules a discussion when both parties can communicate more calmly.

Key point:
- Recognize and regulate your own emotional reactions.
- Consider the other person's emotional state without assuming motives.
- Emotional awareness improves discussion management, not lie-detection certainty.

Source links:
- Investigative Interviewing — Oxford Handbook (2023)
- Eliciting Reliable Information in Investigative Interviews — Vrij, Hope and Fisher (2014)
20. Q: How should intuition be used when someone feels that another person's explanation is incomplete or suspicious?
A: Show answer
Intuition can draw attention to inconsistencies that deserve further examination. It may reflect previous experience, familiarity with a person's behavior, or awareness of details that have not yet been consciously organized. However, intuition can also be affected by prejudice, anxiety, personal conflict, and confirmation bias. It should therefore be treated as a starting point for questions rather than a final conclusion. A useful approach is to identify what specifically caused the concern and determine whether supporting evidence exists. This allows personal impressions to guide investigation without replacing objective verification.

Practical scenario: A project officer feels uncomfortable about a supplier's explanation for a delivery delay. Rather than assuming dishonesty, the officer checks the delivery schedule, correspondence, and transport documentation.

Key point:
- Intuition can identify issues worth examining.
- Personal feelings must be tested against evidence.
- Avoid treating a strong impression as proof.

Source links:
- Accuracy of Deception Judgments — Bond and DePaulo (2006)
- Truth-Default Theory and the Psychology of Lying and Deception Detection — Levine (2022)
21. Q: Why should the content and context of a person's explanation receive more attention than isolated nonverbal signals?
A: Show answer
The content of an explanation can be compared with dates, records, documents, previous statements, and independently verifiable events. Nonverbal signals such as eye movements, facial expressions, posture, or nervousness do not reliably identify dishonesty across different people and situations. Context is essential because the same behavior can have different explanations. A useful assessment examines whether the account is consistent with reliable evidence and whether important information has been omitted. Research on deception detection emphasizes the limitations of relying on behavioral cues and supports approaches that examine communication content and relevant contextual information.

Practical scenario: An auditor is reviewing an explanation for a missing receipt. Instead of judging the employee's facial expression, the auditor checks the transaction date, payment amount, approval history, and alternative supporting records.

Key point:
- Evidence and context are more useful than isolated body-language observations.
- Behavioral cues should not be interpreted as conclusive indicators.
- Examine the substance of the explanation.

Source links:
- Truth-Default Theory — Levine (2014)
- Cues to Deception — DePaulo et al. (2003)
- Active Deception Detection — Levine (2014)
22. Q: What does the truth-default concept explain about why people frequently accept statements without questioning them?
A: Show answer
Truth-default theory proposes that people generally tend to treat communication as truthful unless circumstances activate suspicion or doubt. This tendency supports ordinary social interaction because questioning every statement would make everyday communication inefficient and exhausting. However, it can also make people vulnerable when a person repeatedly provides misleading information or when important warning signs are ignored. The solution is not to become suspicious of everyone. It is to develop appropriate verification practices when a statement has significant consequences or conflicts with reliable evidence. This balance allows trust to remain useful while ensuring that important decisions are based on adequate information.

Practical scenario: A project manager normally accepts routine progress updates from the team. When a major claim conflicts with procurement records and delivery confirmations, the manager conducts additional verification before approving the report.

Key point:
- Everyday communication often depends on an initial presumption of honesty.
- Important contradictions should activate verification.
- Healthy trust and appropriate scrutiny should operate together.

Source links:
- Truth-Default Theory and the Psychology of Lying and Deception Detection — Levine (2022)
- Truth-Default Theory — Levine (2014)
23. Q: How can someone distinguish useful fact-checking from an attempt to interrogate or intimidate another person?
A: Show answer
Fact-checking focuses on establishing whether statements match reliable information. Interrogation becomes counterproductive when the person conducting it relies on pressure, intimidation, repeated accusations, or questions designed primarily to obtain agreement. Constructive information gathering uses clear questions, open discussion, relevant documents, and opportunities to correct misunderstandings. The distinction is important because an individual may provide inaccurate information under pressure, even when the original discrepancy was innocent. The objective should be to obtain a complete and accurate account rather than force a confession. Formal investigations should also follow applicable procedures and safeguards.

Practical scenario: During an internal review, the investigator asks an employee to explain how a transaction was authorized, then compares the response with approval records. The employee is not pressured to agree with an unsupported accusation.

Key point:
- Fact-checking examines evidence and consistency.
- Intimidation can damage the reliability of information.
- Information gathering should be structured, fair, and respectful.

Source links:
- Eliciting Reliable Information in Investigative Interviews — Vrij, Hope and Fisher (2014)
- Investigative Interviewing — Oxford Handbook (2023)
24. Q: Why should discrepancies be checked against independent documents and other available evidence?
A: Show answer
Independent verification reduces the risk of basing a decision entirely on one person's explanation or another person's suspicion. Supporting evidence may include contracts, transaction records, correspondence, attendance records, delivery confirmations, approvals, system logs, or third-party confirmations. Each source should be evaluated according to its reliability, relevance, completeness, and timing. Two documents may appear to support the same statement while originating from the same incorrect source, so independent corroboration matters. A proper review should also identify missing evidence and explain any remaining uncertainty rather than claiming a definitive conclusion when the available information is insufficient.

Practical scenario: A supplier claims that a training session was completed. The organization compares the invoice with the approved activity plan, attendance sheet, trainer confirmation, and participant feedback.

Key point:
- Verify important statements using independent evidence.
- Check the reliability and origin of supporting documents.
- Record unresolved contradictions rather than hiding them.

Source links:
- Active Deception Detection — Levine (2014)
- Investigative Interviewing — Oxford Handbook (2023)
25. Q: How should someone evaluate the possible emotional consequences of confronting another person?
A: Show answer
Confrontation can affect both participants emotionally. The person being questioned may experience embarrassment, anger, fear, or anxiety, while the person raising the concern may feel frustration, disappointment, or uncertainty. These reactions can influence how the discussion develops. Before initiating the conversation, consider whether both parties are able to participate constructively and whether the matter is too emotionally charged for immediate discussion. Serious issues should not necessarily be ignored, but choosing an appropriate time and method can reduce unnecessary escalation. The objective is to address the underlying problem without creating additional harm through avoidable personal conflict.

Practical scenario: Two family members disagree about concealed financial information. Instead of discussing the matter during an argument, they agree to revisit it when both can review the relevant records calmly.

Key point:
- Consider the emotional condition of both participants.
- Avoid escalating a discussion unnecessarily.
- Important concerns should still be addressed through an appropriate process.

Source links:
- Eliciting Reliable Information in Investigative Interviews — Vrij, Hope and Fisher (2014)
- Investigative Interviewing — Oxford Handbook (2023)
26. Q: Why should a suspected liar generally not be confronted publicly in front of other people?
A: Show answer
Public confrontation can increase embarrassment and encourage someone to focus on protecting their reputation instead of explaining what happened. An audience may also influence how participants respond because each person may feel pressure to defend their position. Private discussions can reduce unnecessary social pressure and make it easier to examine sensitive information. However, privacy should not be confused with secrecy that bypasses formal oversight. Serious workplace misconduct may require an authorized investigation, documented interviews, and appropriate witnesses. The correct setting depends on the issue, the organization's procedures, and the safety of everyone involved.

Practical scenario: A manager notices that an employee's explanation conflicts with a project report. The manager does not challenge the employee during a public team meeting but arranges a formal private discussion with appropriate documentation.

Key point:
- Avoid public humiliation and unnecessary audience pressure.
- Use private discussions for appropriate interpersonal clarification.
- Follow formal investigation procedures for serious issues.

Source links:
- Investigative Interviewing — Oxford Handbook (2023)
- Eliciting Reliable Information in Investigative Interviews — Vrij, Hope and Fisher (2014)
27. Q: How should someone handle suspected dishonesty involving a person who has greater professional authority or power?
A: Show answer
Power differences can make confrontation risky. An employee challenging a senior manager may face professional consequences even when the concern is legitimate. The appropriate approach is to assess the evidence, review applicable organizational policies, and select a suitable reporting or escalation channel. Depending on the circumstances, this could involve an authorized supervisor, compliance officer, ethics function, safeguarding contact, internal audit team, or external authority. Direct confrontation is not always the safest or most effective first step. Documenting events accurately and preserving relevant evidence can help protect the integrity of the process and reduce the risk of retaliation or uncontrolled disclosure.

Practical scenario: An employee suspects that a senior manager misrepresented a procurement decision. Rather than making a public accusation, the employee preserves the relevant records and uses the organization's authorized reporting channel.

Key point:
- Consider power imbalances before confronting someone.
- Use formal reporting channels where appropriate.
- Protect evidence and personal safety.

Source links:
- Investigative Interviewing — Oxford Handbook (2023)
- Eliciting Reliable Information in Investigative Interviews — Vrij, Hope and Fisher (2014)
28. Q: Why might a person continue denying a lie even after apparently strong evidence has been presented?
A: Show answer
Denial can occur for different reasons, including fear of consequences, shame, concern about reputation, self-protection, disagreement about the interpretation of events, or an unwillingness to accept responsibility. The person may also believe that their explanation is accurate because they remember events differently. Repeated denial does not automatically prove the original suspicion, although it may indicate that the conversation is not progressing. A constructive response should return to the specific facts, explain the unresolved concern, and consider whether additional verification or a different process is necessary. Understanding possible reasons for denial helps avoid an automatic escalation into anger or intimidation.

Practical scenario: An employee continues denying responsibility for an incomplete financial report. The manager identifies the specific missing approvals and asks the employee to explain the records rather than repeatedly demanding an admission.

Key point:
- Denial can have several psychological and factual explanations.
- Do not assume that denial alone proves deception.
- Return to verifiable evidence and unresolved issues.

Source links:
- Truth-Default Theory and the Psychology of Lying and Deception Detection — Levine (2022)
- Investigative Interviewing — Oxford Handbook (2023)
29. Q: How can someone remain calm when the other person repeatedly denies an explanation that conflicts with the available evidence?
A: Show answer
Remaining calm helps prevent the discussion from becoming a competition over emotional intensity. When someone repeatedly denies a concern, the person asking questions should avoid shouting, issuing personal insults, or making increasingly extreme accusations. Instead, restate the specific discrepancy, identify the evidence, and explain why the issue remains unresolved. If the discussion becomes unproductive, it may be better to pause and arrange another meeting or use an appropriate formal process. Emotional control does not mean accepting an inaccurate explanation. It means maintaining the ability to assess the facts and choose a proportionate next step.

Practical scenario: A supplier denies receiving an advance payment. The finance officer calmly provides the bank reference and payment date, asks the supplier to check the account records, and documents the unresolved issue if disagreement continues.

Key point:
- Regulate emotions during disagreement.
- Repeat the specific factual concern without personal attacks.
- Pause or escalate when the discussion stops producing useful information.

Source links:
- Eliciting Reliable Information in Investigative Interviews — Vrij, Hope and Fisher (2014)
- Investigative Interviewing — Oxford Handbook (2023)
30. Q: How can someone provide an opportunity for another person to correct a false statement without encouraging further deception?
A: Show answer
A constructive conversation can allow someone to explain a mistake or correct inaccurate information without unnecessary humiliation. This may reduce the pressure to maintain a false account. However, providing an opportunity to clarify should not involve promising immunity from reasonable consequences or ignoring the original impact. The person should be clear that the objective is to establish the facts and resolve the issue. An appropriate correction may require amended documentation, an apology, repayment, formal disclosure, or another corrective action. The opportunity to explain is valuable because it improves the chance of obtaining accurate information, but it must be accompanied by accountability.

Practical scenario: A project officer submits an inaccurate expenditure statement. The finance manager invites the officer to explain the error and submit corrected supporting records while preserving the original submission and approval history.

Key point:
- Allow a reasonable opportunity for clarification and correction.
- Preserve original records and document changes.
- Do not confuse a chance to explain with automatic exemption from accountability.

Source links:
- Eliciting Reliable Information in Investigative Interviews — Vrij, Hope and Fisher (2014)
- Investigative Interviewing — Oxford Handbook (2023)
31. Q: Why should someone reconsider their own approach when a conversation about suspected dishonesty produces no progress?
A: Show answer
A discussion may become unproductive because the other person refuses to cooperate, but the person conducting the conversation should also evaluate their own behavior. Consider whether the questions were clear, whether sufficient information was presented, whether the discussion became accusatory, and whether the other person had a genuine opportunity to respond. Personal assumptions and confirmation bias can make someone interpret every explanation as further evidence of guilt. Reassessing the approach can identify misunderstandings or weaknesses in the original reasoning. This does not require abandoning a legitimate concern. It means being prepared to revise an interpretation when the evidence or circumstances justify it.

Practical scenario: A manager believes that an employee deliberately falsified attendance records. After reviewing additional information, the manager discovers that the reporting system had an incorrect date configuration and adjusts the investigation accordingly.

Key point:
- Examine your own assumptions as well as the other person's statements.
- Review the quality of the questions and supporting evidence.
- Change your assessment when reliable new information becomes available.

Source links:
- Investigative Interviewing — Oxford Handbook (2023)
- Accuracy of Deception Judgments — Bond and DePaulo (2006)
32. Q: When should someone stop trying to obtain an admission from a person who continues to deny suspected dishonesty?
A: Show answer
A conversation should not continue indefinitely simply because the other person refuses to provide the desired answer. When reasonable attempts to clarify the issue have been exhausted, it may be appropriate to stop the direct discussion and decide on another course of action. That could involve accepting that the truth cannot currently be established, maintaining reasonable boundaries, seeking independent evidence, or referring the matter to a formal process. In some personal situations, protecting emotional well-being may be more useful than repeatedly demanding an admission. In serious professional or legal matters, however, stopping a personal conversation does not necessarily mean abandoning formal responsibilities.

Practical scenario: A person repeatedly asks a former friend to admit that they concealed important information. After several unsuccessful discussions, the person stops the argument, establishes communication boundaries, and makes future decisions based on the information that can be verified.

Key point:
- Recognize when direct discussion is no longer productive.
- An admission is not always necessary to establish appropriate boundaries.
- Serious risks may still require formal reporting or investigation.

Source links:
- Truth-Default Theory and the Psychology of Lying and Deception Detection — Levine (2022)
- Investigative Interviewing — Oxford Handbook (2023)
33. Q: How can someone protect their emotional well-being when dealing with repeated deception?
A: Show answer
Repeatedly trying to establish the truth can become emotionally exhausting, particularly when the relationship is important or the other person remains unwilling to communicate honestly. Protecting emotional well-being involves recognizing personal limits, deciding which matters require attention, and refusing to make every interaction a debate about honesty. It may be necessary to reduce dependence on the person's statements, establish clearer expectations, or change the nature of the relationship. In a professional context, boundaries can include written approvals, independent verification, and restricted authority. Protecting oneself is not the same as seeking revenge; it is a reasonable response to repeated uncertainty and harmful behavior.

Practical scenario: A person discovers repeated financial misrepresentations by a business partner. They stop relying on informal assurances, require documented approvals, and obtain independent professional advice before continuing the relationship.

Key point:
- Recognize emotional limits and prioritize important issues.
- Establish boundaries where trust has been damaged.
- Use appropriate safeguards instead of relying entirely on verbal assurances.

Source links:
- Truth-Default Theory and the Psychology of Lying and Deception Detection — Levine (2022)
- Investigative Interviewing — Oxford Handbook (2023)
34. Q: What does it mean to outsmart a liar, and why should it involve verification rather than manipulation?
A: Show answer
Dealing effectively with deception does not require using tricks, intimidation, or deliberate counter-deception. A more reliable approach is to reduce dependence on unsupported statements and create a process in which important information can be verified. This includes asking clear questions, preserving records, comparing independent evidence, and maintaining appropriate boundaries. When someone repeatedly misrepresents information, the response should focus on preventing further harm rather than trying to defeat the person psychologically. In professional settings, strong controls are more dependable than relying on an individual's ability to recognize lies through intuition or body language.

Practical scenario: A nonprofit organization suspects that a partner has overstated project expenditure. Rather than attempting to trap the partner with misleading questions, the organization reconciles invoices, payment records, activity evidence, and the partner's financial report.

Key point:
- Verification is more reliable than psychological tricks.
- Maintain evidence and strengthen relevant controls.
- Protect decision-making from unsupported claims.

Source links:
- Active Deception Detection — Levine (2014)
- Eliciting Reliable Information in Investigative Interviews — Vrij, Hope and Fisher (2014)
35. Q: What should someone do when another person uses similar confrontation techniques to question their own honesty?
A: Show answer
Being questioned about honesty should not automatically be interpreted as an attack. The person being questioned should identify the concern, understand what information is being challenged, and provide an accurate explanation supported by relevant evidence. If a mistake occurred, acknowledging it and explaining the corrective action is generally more constructive than inventing additional explanations. At the same time, the person has the right to challenge unfair accusations and ask for specific evidence. Constructive communication requires both participants to remain open to clarification. The discussion should therefore provide an opportunity for mutual understanding rather than becoming a one-sided attempt to establish guilt.

Practical scenario: A project manager questions a staff member about a missing receipt. The staff member explains the transaction, provides an alternative payment record, acknowledges that the original receipt was lost, and agrees to follow the organization's documentation procedure.

Key point:
- Respond to specific concerns rather than reacting only to accusations.
- Provide accurate supporting information.
- Acknowledge mistakes and correct them transparently.

Source links:
- Eliciting Reliable Information in Investigative Interviews — Vrij, Hope and Fisher (2014)
- Investigative Interviewing — Oxford Handbook (2023)
36. Q: How should trust be rebuilt after someone admits to making a false statement?
A: Show answer
An admission is an important development, but it does not automatically restore trust. Rebuilding trust requires acknowledging what happened, explaining the relevant circumstances, accepting responsibility, correcting the consequences, and demonstrating more reliable behavior over time. The affected person should consider the seriousness of the incident, whether the admission was voluntary, whether the person has shown genuine accountability, and whether similar behavior has occurred before. In professional relationships, trust may need to be supported by additional controls even after an apology. The process should include measurable changes rather than relying exclusively on promises that the behavior will never happen again.

Practical scenario: A team member admits to submitting incorrect project figures. The organization requires corrected reports, documents the incident, reviews the cause, and introduces independent checks before the next reporting cycle.

Key point:
- An admission is not the same as restored trust.
- Corrective actions must address the original consequences.
- Consistent future behavior is necessary to demonstrate reliability.

Source links:
- Eliciting Reliable Information in Investigative Interviews — Vrij, Hope and Fisher (2014)
- Investigative Interviewing — Oxford Handbook (2023)
37. Q: How should an organization distinguish an isolated inaccurate statement from a repeated pattern of workplace deception?
A: Show answer
A single inaccurate statement may result from misunderstanding, incomplete information, or an isolated mistake. A repeated pattern requires closer examination because it may indicate systematic concealment, weaknesses in internal controls, or deliberate misrepresentation. The assessment should consider the frequency of incidents, their consistency, the benefits obtained, the consequences, and whether previous corrective measures were ignored. Documented examples are more useful than general descriptions such as "this person is always dishonest." A pattern should be established through evidence gathered across multiple incidents and evaluated fairly. Appropriate responses may include stronger supervision, revised authorization procedures, formal investigation, or disciplinary action under applicable policy.

Practical scenario: A finance department notices that the same employee repeatedly reports supplier payments as completed before the bank transactions have been processed. Management reviews the incident history and introduces independent verification before payment completion is recorded.

Key point:
- Evaluate repeated incidents using documented evidence.
- Distinguish isolated errors from recurring misconduct.
- Strengthen controls when the pattern creates operational or financial risk.

Source links:
- Investigative Interviewing — Oxford Handbook (2023)
- Truth-Default Theory and the Psychology of Lying and Deception Detection — Levine (2022)
38. Q: How can a neutral, evidence-based conversation be structured when a colleague's explanation conflicts with project records?
A: Show answer
Begin by identifying the specific statement that requires clarification and the records that appear inconsistent with it. Explain the concern without accusing the colleague of deliberate deception. Invite the colleague to describe what happened, listen to the complete explanation, and ask open-ended follow-up questions about the timeline and supporting documents. Compare the explanation with independently available evidence and identify any remaining differences. If an error is confirmed, determine the appropriate correction and document the actions agreed upon. If deliberate misrepresentation remains a reasonable concern, use the organization's formal investigation process rather than attempting to obtain a confession through pressure.

Practical scenario: A project coordinator states that all training activities were completed, but attendance sheets and venue records indicate that one session may not have taken place. The manager requests an explanation and verifies the remaining documentation before approving the report.

Key point:
- Identify the exact discrepancy.
- Listen and ask neutral questions.
- Verify the explanation against records.
- Document corrective or investigative action.

Source links:
- Eliciting Reliable Information in Investigative Interviews — Vrij, Hope and Fisher (2014)
- Active Deception Detection — Levine (2014)
39. Q: What does the historical story of Pope Joan illustrate about deception, historical uncertainty, and the importance of evidence?
A: Show answer
The story of Pope Joan concerns an alleged woman who supposedly disguised herself as a man and became pope during the medieval period. The account became popular in later historical writing, but there is no reliable contemporary evidence establishing that such a papacy occurred. Modern historical scholarship examines when the story first appeared, how it was transmitted, and how religious and political controversies influenced its interpretation. Research published in the 2025 Cambridge History of the Papacy discusses how stories about a female pope began circulating in the thirteenth century and how later writers used the narrative for different purposes. The example illustrates why an interesting story should not automatically be treated as a verified historical fact.

Practical scenario: A researcher finds several websites repeating the Pope Joan story. Instead of treating repetition as confirmation, the researcher examines the earliest identifiable accounts, their historical dates, the reliability of their authors, and the absence of contemporary records.

Key point:
- A popular historical story is not necessarily an established fact.
- The date and reliability of original sources matter.
- Repeated retelling does not automatically establish historical accuracy.

Source links:
- The Myth of Pope Joan — Jan Machielsen, The Cambridge History of the Papacy (2025)
- The Myth of Pope Joan — Cardiff University Research Repository
40. Q: Why should historical claims be evaluated according to the quality and timing of their original sources?
A: Show answer
Historical research requires more than collecting stories that agree with one another. Researchers examine contemporary records, the date of later accounts, the author's purpose, the political or religious environment, and whether independent evidence supports the claim. A story written centuries after an alleged event must be treated differently from an authenticated document produced at the time. Later writers may repeat earlier legends, misunderstand events, or adapt narratives for political and cultural purposes. The Pope Joan legend illustrates how a story can become influential even when its historical basis is disputed. The same principle applies to everyday judgments about dishonesty: the origin, reliability, and independence of the information matter.

Practical scenario: A researcher reviews an alleged historical event supported only by accounts written several centuries later. They distinguish the evidence of the later legend from the evidence needed to establish that the event actually occurred.

Key point:
- Evaluate sources according to their date, origin, and reliability.
- Distinguish contemporary evidence from later storytelling.
- Independent corroboration strengthens historical conclusions.

Source links:
- The Myth of Pope Joan — Cambridge University Press (2025)
- The Myth of Pope Joan — Cardiff University
41. Q: What is the practical difference between passive lie detection and active, evidence-based deception assessment?
A: Show answer
Passive lie detection involves watching someone's behavior or listening to their statements and attempting to decide whether they are lying. This approach is limited because many behavioral indicators are unreliable and people vary in how they communicate. Active, evidence-based assessment involves gathering relevant information, asking carefully selected questions, checking claims against independent records, and examining inconsistencies. This approach is more structured because it focuses on information that can be evaluated rather than relying entirely on impressions. It still requires professional judgment because interviews may be affected by memory problems, misunderstanding, incomplete evidence, and interviewer bias.

Practical scenario: Instead of deciding whether a contractor is dishonest based on nervous behavior, a project manager asks for a detailed work timeline and compares it with the contract, delivery records, and independent confirmation.

Key point:
- Passive observation has important accuracy limitations.
- Active assessment uses questions and independent verification.
- Neither method should be treated as infallible.

Source links:
- Active Deception Detection — Levine (2014)
- Cues to Deception — DePaulo et al. (2003)
42. Q: How can organizations reduce the risk of being misled by inaccurate or deliberately false reports?
A: Show answer
Organizations should establish systems that make important claims independently verifiable. These systems may include separation of duties, approval controls, reconciliations, documented procedures, supporting-document requirements, access controls, and periodic independent reviews. A statement from an employee or partner should not automatically be treated as proof that a transaction or activity has occurred. Important reports should be compared with available transaction evidence and operational records. The strength of the control environment matters because it reduces opportunities for concealment and makes discrepancies easier to identify. Effective controls also protect honest staff from being wrongly accused because decisions are based on documented processes rather than personal suspicion.

Practical scenario: A nonprofit introduces a requirement that project completion reports be supported by activity records, approved budgets, payment evidence, and independent review before management signs off on donor reporting.

Key point:
- Build verification into important organizational processes.
- Separate preparation, review, and approval responsibilities.
- Use documentation and reconciliation to reduce dependence on verbal assurances.

Source links:
- Investigative Interviewing — Oxford Handbook (2023)
- Active Deception Detection — Levine (2014)
43. Q: How should someone manage the difference between wanting the truth and protecting an important personal relationship?
A: Show answer
The desire for complete honesty may conflict with the desire to preserve a meaningful relationship. The correct decision depends on the significance of the information, the degree of harm caused, the expectations within the relationship, and the possibility of resolving the issue. Sometimes an uncomfortable but respectful discussion can strengthen a relationship because both parties understand what happened. In other situations, repeatedly pursuing a minor personal matter may create more damage than benefit. The person should therefore decide what information is essential, what boundaries are necessary, and whether the relationship can continue under more realistic expectations. Protecting a relationship should not require ignoring serious harm or accepting repeated manipulation.

Practical scenario: A person discovers that a close friend concealed a personal decision. They discuss the effect on their relationship and agree on clearer communication expectations rather than allowing the disagreement to develop into repeated accusations.

Key point:
- Consider both honesty and relationship consequences.
- Preserve boundaries without demanding unnecessary disclosure.
- Serious deception should not be ignored solely to avoid conflict.

Source links:
- Truth-Default Theory and the Psychology of Lying and Deception Detection — Levine (2022)
- Eliciting Reliable Information in Investigative Interviews — Vrij, Hope and Fisher (2014)
44. Q: What is a practical step-by-step framework for confronting suspected dishonesty constructively?
A: Show answer
A structured approach can help keep a difficult conversation fair and focused. First, identify the specific statement or event that created concern. Second, verify the available evidence and consider alternative explanations. Third, evaluate the seriousness and consequences of the issue. Fourth, select a suitable time, location, and communication channel. Fifth, describe the concern without immediately accusing the person. Sixth, invite an explanation through open-ended questions and listen carefully. Seventh, compare the explanation with available evidence and clarify unresolved differences. Finally, determine whether the matter requires correction, accountability, stronger safeguards, formal escalation, or no further action. The approach should remain proportionate to the seriousness of the issue and the responsibilities of the people involved.

Practical scenario: A manager receives an inaccurate procurement report. They identify the discrepancy, verify the underlying invoice and approval records, arrange a private meeting, request an explanation, reconcile the facts, and document the corrective decision.

Key point:
- Verify before confronting.
- Communicate respectfully and ask open questions.
- Evaluate evidence, consequences, and appropriate corrective action.
- Document material decisions and preserve legitimate accountability.

Source links:
- Eliciting Reliable Information in Investigative Interviews — Vrij, Hope and Fisher (2014)
- Investigative Interviewing — Oxford Handbook (2023)
- Active Deception Detection — Levine (2014)
45. Q: What overall lesson does this chapter provide about dealing with dishonesty and maintaining trust?
A: Show answer
The central lesson is that dealing with dishonesty requires more than identifying contradictions or demanding admissions. It requires understanding possible motivations, examining facts, managing emotions, asking constructive questions, evaluating consequences, and recognizing personal limits. Trust should not be abandoned automatically whenever uncertainty arises, but neither should it depend entirely on unsupported assurances. Healthy relationships and effective organizations combine open communication with reasonable verification and accountability. Some issues can be resolved through empathy and clarification, while others require stronger boundaries or formal intervention. The most effective response is therefore proportionate, evidence-based, and guided by the actual consequences of the behavior rather than an emotional need to expose every perceived lie.

Practical scenario: A department discovers repeated discrepancies in project reporting. Rather than relying on personal accusations, management improves documentation, introduces independent reviews, discusses identified issues constructively, and establishes accountability for future reporting.

Key point:
- Understanding motives improves the quality of communication.
- Evidence protects against both deception and false accusations.
- Empathy should coexist with accountability.
- Trust is maintained through consistent behavior and appropriate verification.

Source links:
- Accuracy of Deception Judgments — Bond and DePaulo (2006)
- Truth-Default Theory and the Psychology of Lying and Deception Detection — Levine (2022)
- Eliciting Reliable Information in Investigative Interviews — Vrij, Hope and Fisher (2014)
